beta release/vX.Y bi-weekly early adopters
nightly main HEAD daily artifact CI integration
  • Breaking API changes require major version bump + migration guide in CHANGELOG.md.
  • mem-api crate maintains semver compatibility; breaking changes gated behind feature flags for one release cycle.

Performance Engineering

Benchmarking Strategy

  • mem-bench crate: Criterion.rs for micro/meso; custom harness for end-to-end throughput + latency.
  • Quantified performance targets (M1…), each gated in CI (PR merge blocked on regression beyond the stated threshold vs bench-baseline.json):
# Metric Target (baseline) Phase Gate
M1 remember_throughput — records/s, single-threaded ≥ 500 rec/s (incl. embed mock) §18 (Phase 6) CI
M2 remember_p99 — latency ≤ 20 ms (WAL fsync=async) §13 (Phase 1) CI
M3 recall_p50 — query latency ≤ 5 ms (1 M records, k=10) §16 (Phase 4) CI
M4 recall_p99 — query latency ≤ 30 ms §16 (Phase 4) CI
M5 hnsw_recall_at_10 — ANN recall quality ≥ 0.95 §15 (Phase 3) CI
M6 bm25_throughput — queries/s ≥ 2 000 QPS §15 (Phase 3) CI
M7 consolidation_throughput — records consolidated/s ≥ 100/s §20 (Phase 8) CI
M8 context_assemble_p50 — assembly latency ≤ 2 ms (budget=4 096 tokens) §19 (Phase 7) CI
M9 wal_write_throughput — bytes/s ≥ 100 MB/s sequential §13 (Phase 1) CI
M10 soak_rss_growth_1h — RSS growth after plateau ≤ 50 MB/h §25 (Phase 13) nightly
M11 differential_overlap_at_10 — HNSW vs brute-force overlap@10 ≥ 0.90 at 100 k corpus §25 (Phase 13) CI
  • Benchmarks isolated: pin to CPU core (taskset), disable Turbo, governor=performance.
  • Statistical: Criterion reports mean ± σ, outlier detection; minimum 100 iterations, 5 s measurement window.
  • Benchmark inputs: pre-generated fixtures in mem-bench/fixtures/; reproducible via seeded RNG.

Profiling Methodology

  • CPU profiling: cargo flamegraph (perf + inferno) on hottest benchmark; record → flamegraph.svg per crate.
  • Heap profiling: heaptrack or dhat (via dhat-heap feature flag); track peak RSS, live bytes, allocation sites.
  • Allocation tracing: #[global_allocator] = tikv_jemallocator in release; jemalloc stats dumped via mem_api::stats().
  • Lock contention: perf lock or locking-report feature in tokio; identify hot mutexes in mem-store and mem-index.
  • I/O profiling: strace -c or blktrace for WAL write patterns; target: sequential writes, minimal seeks.
  • Cache analysis: perf stat -e cache-misses,cache-references on HNSW search; L3 miss rate target ≤ 15%.
  • Profiling runs on dedicated hardware (no shared CI runner); results stored in profiling/ git-ignored; notable regressions documented in ADR.

Flamegraph Workflow

Step Tool Invocation Purpose
1 cargo bench --bench recall_bench --no-run Build the benchmark binary without executing it
2 cargo flamegraph --bench recall_bench -- --bench Run under perf/inferno, emit flamegraph.svg
3 Inspect flamegraph.svg Annotate hot frames; compare against the checklist below
  • Hot-frame checklist: hnsw::search (dist computations), bm25::score (postings decode), wal::append (fsync), rrf::fuse (sort + merge).
  • Regression: new hot frame > 5% of total CPU triggers investigation issue.

Tracing Methodology

  • tracing crate (structured spans); mem-telemetry wires up tracing-opentelemetry exporter.
  • Span taxonomy: remember.root, ingest.extract, ingest.embed, store.wal_append, retrieve.hybrid, retrieve.rrf, retrieve.mmr, context.assemble, consolidation.pass.
  • Trace-driven latency breakdown: p99_budget_analysis script parses OTEL traces; identifies which span dominates tail latency.
  • Sampling: 100% in test/bench; configurable in production (default 1% with head-based + tail-based for errors/slow requests).

Allocator and Lock Analysis

  • jemalloc as default allocator (feature jemalloc); mimalloc as alternative (feature mimalloc); benchmark both.
  • Allocation hot paths: avoid per-record Vec allocation in retrieval hot path; use arena (bumpalo) for temporary scoring buffers.
  • Lock hierarchy (strict): store_lock → index_lock → cache_lock; no reverse acquisition; verified by parking_lot deadlock detector in test builds.
  • RwLock preferred over Mutex for read-heavy index paths; upgrade path: index_lock.read() → drop → index_lock.write() with re-check.
  • Syscall minimization: WAL uses write_vectored (scatter-gather); mmap avoids per-record read() syscall; io_uring considered in ADR (deferred).

Throughput and Tail-Latency Targets

Scenario p50 p95 p99 p999
remember() warm (embed cached) 3 ms 8 ms 15 ms 50 ms
remember() cold (embed call) LlmIface-bound — — —
recall() 1 M records k=10 5 ms 15 ms 30 ms 100 ms
context assemble 4 096 tokens 1 ms 3 ms 5 ms 20 ms
forget() single record < 1 ms 2 ms 5 ms 15 ms
  • Tail-latency drivers: HNSW graph lock contention, WAL fsync flush, LlmIface RTT; each tracked independently via spans.
  • SLO breach alert: p99 > 2× target triggers PagerDuty-equivalent alert; tracked in mem-telemetry histograms.

Memory Profiling

  • RSS budget: 1 M records + 768-dim embeddings ≈ 3 GB; warn if RSS exceeds 1.2× this at steady state.
  • Embedding storage: quantization (f16 fallback) available via feature flag; reduces embedding storage ~2×.
  • HNSW graph RAM: O(M × n) edges; M=16 default; 1 M nodes × 16 × 4 bytes ≈ 64 MB; documented in capacity planning table.
  • Working-memory cap: ContextBudget.max_tokens × 4 bytes per token upper-bound; eviction enforced before this ceiling.

Scalability Analysis

Bottleneck Taxonomy

Bottleneck Subsystem Symptom Mitigation
WAL fsync throughput mem-store remember() p99 spikes batched fsync, group commit
HNSW write lock mem-index recall degrades under concurrent ingest read/write index versioning, copy-on-write segments
Embedding RTT mem-embed ingest pipeline stalls async pipeline, embed cache, batching
LlmIface RTT (extract/summarize) mem-ingest, mem-consolidate consolidation lag async job queue, rate-limit-aware scheduler
BM25 postings decode mem-index high-QPS BM25 CPU SIMD decode, compressed postings (VBYTE)
Compaction I/O mem-store read amplification during compaction level-triggered compaction, rate limiter
Context assembly sort mem-context latency scales with working-memory size cap working-memory size, priority heap
Metadata index scans mem-index filter queries on large namespaces secondary B-tree index per namespace

Scaling Ceilings (single-node)

Resource Ceiling Notes
HNSW recall quality degrades at ~10 M nodes with M=16 increase M or segment index
WAL file size recommend compaction trigger at 1 GB WAL controls replay time on crash
Embedding store (mmap) OS-limited virtual address space; practical ~50 GB (f32) f16 doubles ceiling
BM25 vocabulary ~5 M unique terms before posting-list seek cost rises aggressive stop-wording
Working memory max_tokens × token_size; soft cap 64 K tokens active evict to episodic above cap
Concurrent writers write lock on WAL; practical ceiling ~200 concurrent ingests shard by namespace

Horizontal Scaling

  • Namespace sharding: each NsId assigned to a shard (consistent hash ring); no cross-shard queries in baseline design.
  • Read replicas: snapshot + WAL streaming replication; replicas handle recall(); writes go to primary.
  • Embedding service scale-out: mem-embed provider is stateless HTTP; horizontally scalable independently.
  • Consolidation workers: stateless except for LlmIface calls; scale out independently; job queue in mem-store (deduped by namespace + job type).
  • Query fan-out: optional multi-shard recall fan-out + merge; RRF fusion applies across shard results; coordination overhead O(shards × k).

Vertical Scaling

  • HNSW search is CPU-bound; additional cores improve QPS linearly up to ~16 cores (then lock contention dominates).
  • WAL throughput scales with I/O bandwidth; NVMe SSD target; RAID-0 for write throughput.
  • Embedding batch size: larger batch → higher GPU/CPU utilization; diminishing returns above batch=256.
  • mmap benefits from large RAM; OS page cache absorbs hot records; cold-record access latency drops with more RAM.

Replication and Consistency

  • Replication model: primary → replicas via WAL segment shipping; replay on replica.
  • Consistency: eventual read-your-writes within a session (session pinned to primary or replica with WAL lag < 500 ms threshold).
  • Failover: replica promotion; promote replica with smallest WAL lag; max data loss window = replication lag.
  • Split-brain prevention: single primary per shard; no multi-master; fencing via lease renewal (TTL 5 s, heartbeat 1 s).

Coordination Overhead

  • Consolidation job dedup: (namespace, job_type) key prevents duplicate jobs; atomic CAS in mem-store job table.
  • Distributed forget(): fan-out to all shards holding namespace records; two-phase: mark tombstone → compact.
  • Namespace metadata: central registry (small, O(1 K) namespaces); cached with TTL 30 s on each node.
  • HNSW cross-shard ANN: not supported in baseline; each shard maintains independent index; cross-shard similarity requires scatter-gather.

Storage Growth and Metadata Scaling

  • Raw growth rate: records/day × avg_record_size; 100 K records/day × 2 KB avg = 200 MB/day raw (pre-compaction).
  • Post-compaction ratio: ~3× compression typical (text + quantized embeddings); target ≤ 70 MB/day steady state.
  • WAL retention: keep last 2 snapshots + WAL since older snapshot; older WAL GC'd after snapshot confirmed.
  • Index growth: HNSW O(n log n) edges; BM25 O(n × avg_terms) postings; metadata B-tree O(n log n).
  • Tiering triggers: hot (accessed < 7 d), warm (7–90 d), cold (> 90 d or salience < 0.05); cold tier moved to compressed archive; hot tier kept in mmap.
  • Capacity planning table:
Records Embedding store (f32 768-d) HNSW index BM25 index Total approx
100 K 300 MB 50 MB 20 MB ~370 MB
1 M 3 GB 500 MB 200 MB ~3.7 GB
10 M 30 GB 5 GB 2 GB ~37 GB

Scheduler Saturation

  • Tokio runtime: default worker threads = num_cpus; consolidation tasks must not monopolize executor — use spawn_blocking for CPU-bound HNSW inserts.
  • Background task budget: consolidation + forget + compaction capped at 25% CPU via token-bucket rate limiter on job dispatch.
  • Backpressure: ingest observation buffer bounded (configurable, default 10 K); remember() blocks with timeout if buffer full → returns MemError::BufferFull.
  • LlmIface concurrency: bounded semaphore (default 8 concurrent requests); configurable; avoids provider rate-limit errors cascading into ingest stalls.

Network Fanout (distributed mode)

  • Recall fan-out: N shards → N parallel gRPC requests; merge timeout = min(shard_p99 + 10 ms, 200 ms); missing shard returns partial results (logged, not error).
  • Embedding provider: HTTP/2 multiplexing; connection pool per provider instance; max pool size = 32.
  • WAL replication: streaming TCP; backpressure via receiver window; lag metric replication_lag_ms exported to telemetry.
  • Metadata propagation: namespace registry gossip; eventual consistency < 1 s typical; stale namespace read returns MemError::NamespaceNotFound → client retries with backoff.

Mental Models & First Principles

MM1 — WAL as the Source of Truth, Store as a Cache

Compressed insight: The record store (mmap slab) is not authoritative — the WAL is. Any in-memory or on-disk record state is derived from a deterministic replay of the WAL. If the store and WAL disagree, the WAL wins.

What it predicts: Compaction can be paused, aborted, or repeated without data loss, because no compaction step destroys WAL entries before a new checkpoint confirms correctness. Recovery time is bounded by WAL segment size, not record store size.

Bug class without it: Engineers treat the record store as the source of truth and optimize it (e.g., in-place mutation of a slot) without writing a WAL entry first. On crash, the mutation is undetectable and the record state is inconsistent. This violates I1 and P2.

Applied in: §13 (Phase 1) WAL layout, §6.2 Crash-Only/Replayability, §7.1 Durability Invariant, §7.8 WAL Append-Only Invariant.

MM2 — HNSW Layers are a Probabilistic Routing Network

Compressed insight: HNSW is a skip-list variant for high-dimensional spaces. Each node appears in zero or more upper layers with exponentially decreasing probability. Upper layers are a coarse routing network; layer 0 is the dense proximity graph. Search always begins at the topmost layer's fixed entry point and greedily descends.

What it predicts: Nodes inserted with a high layer assignment (rare) become long-range connectors that dramatically reduce search path length for all subsequent queries. The ef_construction parameter controls neighbor-candidate set size at build time — too small produces a poorly connected graph; too large slows build without improving recall beyond a ceiling.

Bug class without it: Engineers raise ef_construction indefinitely expecting monotonic recall improvement. After ~300, recall plateaus but build time grows quadratically. Separately, high tombstone ratios (>15%) disconnect routing paths; without this model, engineers don't know why recall degrades without any code change.

Applied in: §15 (Phase 3) HNSW construction, §16.2 (Phase 4) ANN retrieval, ADR-06, PB3 (Malkov & Yashunin 2020).

MM3 — RRF is Score-Scale Agnostic by Construction

Compressed insight: RRF replaces per-result scores with per-result ranks. Because ranks are integers in [1, N], all retrieval lanes contribute equally regardless of whether cosine similarity returns values in [0,1] while BM25 returns unbounded positive reals. The constant k=60 was chosen empirically (Cormack et al. 2009) to dampen the weight of top-ranked results, making the formula robust to one lane completely dominating.

What it predicts: Adding a new retrieval lane (recency, importance) does not require renormalizing any existing lane's scores. A bug that causes BM25 scores to inflate by 100× has zero effect on RRF fusion output.

Bug class without it: Engineers fuse by linear combination of raw scores. BM25 score scale shifts when vocabulary changes (IDF recalculation). The combined score changes, breaking MRR regression tests with no change to the underlying index quality.

Applied in: §16.2 (Phase 4) RRF fusion, §8.14 HybridRetriever, PB4 (Cormack et al. 2009).

MM4 — Embedding-Record Atomicity is a Two-Phase Commit Problem

Compressed insight: Inserting a MemoryRecord requires writing three things: (a) the WAL entry, (b) the record store slot, and (c) the ANN index node. Any subset of these can survive a crash. The only safe crash-recovery protocol is: write WAL first, then record store, then index; on recovery, replay WAL to reconstruct both store and index; detect dangling embedding_refs via mem-cli fsck.

What it predicts: You cannot safely write the embedding to the ANN index before the WAL entry confirms the record. An index node with no corresponding WAL entry cannot be recovered. An embedding_ref in a WAL record with no corresponding ANN node means the record is unretrievable via the vector lane.

Bug class without it: Engineers optimize by writing to the ANN index first (it's fastest). On crash between index write and WAL write, the index contains a vector with no MemoryId in the store. I3 (Embedding–Record Consistency) is violated silently; no error appears until a retrieval returns a MemoryId that the store cannot resolve.

Applied in: §13 (Phase 1) crash recovery, §6.3 Embedding–Record Atomicity, §7.3 Embedding–Record Consistency Invariant.

MM5 — Consolidation Idempotency via derived_from Dedup

Compressed insight: A ConsolidationJob::Summarize over a set of episodic records produces a new Semantic record with Provenance.derived_from listing all source MemoryIds. If the same job replays (crash-restart), the dedup check — "does a record already exist with this derived_from set?" — prevents double-summarization. This makes consolidation a pure function of its inputs.

What it predicts: Consolidation can be safely retried at any point without amplifying the knowledge graph. Reflection records accumulate in derived_from chains that can be traversed to reconstruct any summary's lineage.

Bug class without it: A consolidation job crashes after writing the new Semantic record but before marking the job complete in WAL. On restart, the job replays and creates a second, identical Semantic record. Over time, the knowledge graph accumulates duplicate reflections; semantic retrieval recall degrades because duplicates consume k slots.

Applied in: §20 (Phase 8) SummarizationPipeline, §7.12 Consolidation Idempotency Invariant, I12, PB6 (Sarthi et al. 2024 RAPTOR).

MM6 — Token Budget is a Knapsack with Hard Capacity

Compressed insight: Context assembly is a variant of the 0-1 knapsack problem where items are memory records, weights are token counts, and the capacity is ContextBudget.max_tokens - reserved. The greedy priority heuristic (sort by composite score, greedily pack, skip records that overflow) is O(k log k) and produces near-optimal results because records are small relative to the budget in practice.

What it predicts: A single oversized record (e.g., a verbatim transcript) can exhaust the budget alone. The assembler must detect this case and either truncate the record or skip it (configurable). Lowering reserved increases context richness but reduces LLM headroom for generation.

Bug class without it: Engineers assume records are uniformly small. An agent stores a 10 K token episodic transcript. The assembler packs it as the first item, leaving 0 capacity for any other memories. The agent receives a single giant memory with no cross-reference context, degrading response quality silently.

Applied in: §19 (Phase 7) context assembler, §7.5 Context-Budget Invariant, §6.5 Bounded Resource Usage, PB2 (Packer et al. 2023 MemGPT).

MM7 — Salience Decay is a Forgetting Curve, Not a Timer

Compressed insight: Exponential decay r(t) = exp(-λ·Δt) models the Ebbinghaus forgetting curve: memories fade continuously, not at a discrete expiry point. The composite score D = salience × recency × frequency means high-salience or frequently-accessed records are protected from eviction regardless of age. λ controls the half-life; a record with λ=0.01/hour halves its recency contribution every ~69 hours.

What it predicts: Setting λ too high causes important but rarely-accessed semantic records to fall below eviction threshold quickly. Setting it too low keeps too many stale episodic records in warm storage, bloating the index and degrading retrieval precision. Frequency access patterns can reset effective decay.

Bug class without it: Engineers implement TTL-only eviction. All records older than N days are deleted regardless of access pattern or importance. High-salience semantic knowledge accumulated over months is lost when it hasn't been queried in exactly N+1 days.

Applied in: §21 (Phase 9) decay model, §6.5 Bounded Resource Usage, §7.11 TTL Expiry Invariant, PB1 (Park et al. 2023 recency×importance scoring).

MM8 — Namespace Isolation is a Blast Radius Limiter

Compressed insight: Every operation — WAL entry, record slot, ANN node, BM25 posting — is keyed by NsId. A namespace is not a filter applied at query time; it is a structural partitioning of all storage, indexing, and compaction. Cross-namespace data access requires an explicit join or federation operation (PhaseDeferred 15.x).

What it predicts: A compaction bug in namespace A cannot corrupt namespace B's WAL. A runaway recall query in namespace B cannot exhaust namespace A's index capacity. Namespace deletion is a bounded operation: enumerate all WAL entries with NsId prefix, mark deleted, compact.

Bug class without it: Engineers key records by agent ID only and scope queries via a WHERE clause. A buggy consolidation job in agent X's session writes to a shared inverted index. Agent Y's BM25 recall returns results from agent X's memories.

Applied in: §7.7 Namespace Isolation Invariant, §6.7 Namespace Isolation principle, §23 (Phase 11) namespace management, ADR-12.

Comparative Architecture Table

This design is an embedded, durable, Rust-native memory subsystem. The table below compares it against four real production systems across dimensions that are load-bearing for the stated goals.

Dimension This Design letta-ai/letta (MemGPT) getzep/graphiti (Zep v2) mem0ai/mem0 FAISS + Postgres
Durability model Custom append-only WAL + CRC32c + mmap; crash-safe replay; compaction No WAL; in-memory Python dicts + optional SQLite (no WAL mode) PostgreSQL WAL (Postgres-backed) SQLite without WAL mode; no crash-recovery guarantees Postgres ACID (for metadata); FAISS index snapshotted externally
ANN index Built-in HNSW (pure Rust); pluggable AnnBackend trait None (no vector search; tier by recency only) pgvector inside Postgres Separate vector DB (Qdrant/Chroma/Pinecone); provider-abstract FAISS (production-scale, GPU-optional); no native TTL
Full-text retrieval BM25 inverted index (hand-rolled, in-process) No keyword search Full-text via Postgres tsvector None (embedding-only) Postgres full-text search (GIN index, tsvector)
Hybrid fusion RRF (Cormack 2009) over 4 lanes: vector + BM25 + recency + importance No fusion; single tier by recency BFS + embedding similarity; no formal fusion No fusion No fusion (two separate queries; application merges)
MMR diversity Yes, post-RRF; λ configurable per Query No No No No
Consolidation / reflection Background pipeline: summarize (RAPTOR-style), reflect (Park 2023), merge-dedup, link-graph Explicit LLM function calls for paging; no background consolidation Bi-temporal episode→fact extraction via LLM LLM-based graph update on every write No consolidation
Forgetting / tiering Composite decay (recency × importance × frequency); hot/warm/cold tiers; TTL; eviction audit log Manual delete / context window limit No decay model; graph facts are indefinitely retained unless deleted Configurable TTL only No decay; Postgres rows retained until deleted; FAISS no native TTL
Namespace isolation Structural NsId prefix on all WAL + store + index entries No namespace isolation Partial (per-user graph) No isolation Schema-per-tenant or table-level filtering (not structural)
Language / embedding Rust (edition 2021); abstract LlmIface; no model weight Python; LLM function-call protocol (OpenAI-compatible) Python; OpenAI or BGE embeddings hard-coded Python; many embedding providers C++ (FAISS) + SQL; embedding provider external
Test / bench harness Differential HNSW vs brute-force oracle; nDCG/MRR fixtures; 24 h soak No formal retrieval quality benchmarks No retrieval quality benchmarks No retrieval quality benchmarks FAISS has its own bench (bench_hnsw.py); no memory-system harness

letta-ai/letta (MemGPT): The conceptual parent of this design (Packer et al. 2023). Letta proved that a tiered working/archival split is the right abstraction for in-context memory management, and introduced the paging analogy. This design adopts the tiering concept but replaces Python in-memory dicts with a durable WAL-backed store and adds vector + keyword retrieval to replace Letta's recency-only paging. Letta's monolithic Python Agent class with no namespace isolation or crash recovery is the primary anti-pattern this design avoids.

getzep/graphiti (Zep v2): The closest competitor on knowledge representation. Graphiti's bi-temporal episode→fact graph is a production system with validity/transaction time on every fact node, making it excellent for temporal reasoning. However, it relies on PostgreSQL for all durability (good) but has no decay model and no tier separation — all facts are retained indefinitely. The graph maintenance happens on the hot write path (blocking), making high-throughput ingest impractical. This design defers federation (PhaseDeferred 15.x) but achieves better ingest throughput by decoupling consolidation from the write path.

mem0ai/mem0: A lightweight, developer-facing memory layer with graph augmentation. mem0 has a clean entity dedup pipeline (a model this design adopts for its ingest dedup) and provider-abstract embedding interface (also adopted). However, mem0 uses SQLite without WAL mode and provides no crash-recovery guarantees, no namespace isolation, and no retrieval quality benchmarks. This makes it unsuitable for production agent deployments requiring durability SLAs.

FAISS + Postgres: The simplest production-grade combination: FAISS for vector search, Postgres for metadata and full-text. FAISS provides production-proven HNSW and IVF-PQ at scale (100M+ vectors with GPU acceleration), which this design can only match via the FAISS feature-flag backend (ADR-06). Postgres provides ACID durability and SQL flexibility not available in this design's custom WAL. The combination wins when corpus size exceeds 10M vectors or when the team needs SQL over memory metadata. This design wins when all subsystems must be embedded in a single process, latency to Postgres is unacceptable, or the deployment environment cannot run a separate database process.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All estimates assume: 768-dimension f32 embeddings (standard BERT/sentence-transformer family), average record content length 400 bytes (one or two sentences), k1=1.5/b=0.75 BM25 parameters, HNSW M=16 (16 neighbors per node at layer 0).

# Quantity Formula Result
1 Embedding size per record 768 dims × 4 bytes/f32 3,072 bytes = ~3 KB
2 Content + metadata per record 400 B content + 16 B MemoryId + 8 B timestamps × 2 + 4 B salience + ~100 B provenance + ~50 B links ~580 bytes ≈ 600 B
3 Total on-disk per record (uncompressed) 3,072 B embedding + 600 B record ~3.7 KB/record
4 HNSW edges per node (layer 0) M=16 neighbors × 2 (bidirectional) × 8 B per (MemoryId=16B, score=4B → packed 8B) ~256 bytes/node
5 HNSW index size for 1M records 1,000,000 nodes × 256 B × 1.33 (upper layers overhead factor) ~340 MB
6 BM25 posting list size avg 50 unique terms/record × 1M records × 8 B per posting (MemoryId+tf+field_len) ~400 MB for 1M records
7 Total storage for 100K records 100,000 × 3.7 KB record+embed + 34 MB HNSW + 40 MB BM25 ~444 MB ≈ 450 MB
8 Total storage for 1M records 1,000,000 × 3.7 KB + 340 MB HNSW + 400 MB BM25 ~4.4 GB
9 WAL throughput budget 100 writes/s × 3.7 KB/write × 1.1 (framing overhead) ~410 KB/s → ~35 GB/day raw WAL before compaction
10 WAL after compaction (3:1 ratio) 35 GB / 3 ~12 GB/day net WAL growth
11 p99 recall budget decomposition Vector ANN 10 ms + BM25 2 ms + recency/importance 1 ms + RRF fusion 1 ms + MMR 2 ms + network+serialization 5 ms 21 ms sub-total; 4 ms headroom to p99=25 ms
12 Embedding batch latency batch=32 texts at 50 ms RTT per batch 1.56 ms per record amortized; rate = ~640 embeds/s per provider call

The dominant cost is embeddings: 3 KB out of 3.7 KB per record (83%). Switching to f16 halves the embedding store, yielding ~2.2 GB at 1M records. The HNSW index at 340 MB for 1M records is well within typical server RAM; the practical HNSW ceiling on a 64 GB server is ~188M nodes before memory pressure requires sharding (see Scalability Analysis — Horizontal Scaling, namespace sharding).

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

The following questions require synthesis across multiple sections. Each question names the §§ where the answer lives.

  1. WAL-first invariant under group-commit: If FsyncPolicy::GroupCommit { interval_ms: 5 } is configured and an agent calls remember() during the 5 ms window, at what point exactly is P1 (Durability Before Acknowledgement) satisfied — and what does remember() return before the fsync fires? Trace the code path from remember() through IngestPipeline::observe() to WalWriter::append() and the group-commit tokio task. What happens if the process dies 2 ms into the window? — §§13, 6.1, 7.1

  2. HNSW recall degradation root cause: Tombstone ratio exceeds 20% on a namespace after a mass forget() operation. Retrieval recall@10 measured by the differential harness drops from 0.92 to 0.71. What is the mechanical cause, and what is the minimum set of operations required to restore recall without dropping any live record? — §§15, 8.13, 7.10, 25

  3. RRF k-parameter sensitivity: An operator sets RRF k=10 (instead of the default 60) on a namespace. Explain what happens to the fusion score distribution, which retrieval lane is most affected, and whether MRR@10 is likely to increase or decrease for a corpus where vector similarity scores are highly concentrated near 0.9. — §§16.2, 8.14, 6.4, 4.2

  4. Consolidation and no-silent-loss: A ConsolidationJob::Merge completes: records A and B are merged into C, A and B marked RETIRED. Immediately after, the process crashes before the WAL MarkRetired entries for A and B are written. On recovery, what is the state of the knowledge graph? Does I2 (No-Silent-Loss) hold? How does the system detect and resolve the inconsistency? — §§20, 7.2, 7.12, 13

  5. Cross-crate invariant enforcement: I4 (Retrieval Determinism) states "identical index state + Query → identical ranked set". A new engineer adds a HashMap<MemoryId, f32> intermediate to the RRF fusion step. Why does this break I4, how would the differential harness in Phase 13 detect it, and what is the minimum code change to restore determinism? — §§16.2, 7.4, 25.2, 6.4

  6. Embedding-record atomicity on compaction: During a compaction cycle, the new records.tmp slab is written, but before the atomic rename, the process crashes. On recovery, which of the three files — wal.bin, records.bin, records.tmp — is authoritative, and what is the recovery algorithm? What invariant protects against a partial records.tmp poisoning the store? — §§13, 6.3, 7.3, 7.8

  7. Context budget and working-memory eviction race: Two concurrent recall() calls race with a remember() that pushes working memory over ContextBudget.max_tokens. The eviction path calls mem-ingest::observe() to persist the evicted record. What locks are held, in what order, and is deadlock possible given tokio::sync::RwLock semantics? — §§19, 7.5, 6.5, 12

  8. BM25 IDF stability at small corpus: A namespace has 20 records at project start. BM25 IDF weights for rare terms are extremely high. After 10,000 records are ingested, the same query returns a different ranked set despite no index reconstruction. Is I4 (Retrieval Determinism) violated? Is this a bug? — §§15, 8.13, 7.4, 6.4

  9. Decay lambda calibration: An operator sets lambda_r = 0.001 (per hour) for a namespace expecting memories to remain relevant for weeks. After 30 days, the hot/warm tier contains 850,000 records and warm-storage WAL is 45 GB. What is the expected decay score for a record last accessed 30 days ago with salience=0.5, access_count=3? How should the threshold be adjusted? — §§21, 8.15, 7.9, 7.11

  10. Reflection amplification: A reflection job over 50 episodic records creates 1 semantic record. That semantic record then becomes eligible for a second reflection cycle, creating another semantic record derived from the first. Over 100 consolidation cycles, what is the maximum chain depth of derived_from links? What prevents unbounded reflection amplification? — §§20, 7.12, 6.10, 8

  11. Namespace quota enforcement: NsQuota { max_records: 1_000_000 } is configured. A burst of 2,000 concurrent remember() calls hits the API. The namespace is at 999,990 records. How many succeed, which fail, what error is returned, and is the quota check atomic? — §§23, 7.7, 6.5, 11

  12. HNSW vs BM25 retrieval disagreement: For query Q, the vector ANN lane returns [A, B, C, D, E] and the BM25 lane returns [F, G, H, I, J] — completely disjoint. What does RRF fusion return? Is this a problem, and what does it indicate about the embedding model and/or the BM25 index? — §§16, 6.4, 8.14, 15

  13. WAL segment rotation and recovery time bound: wal_max_bytes = 512 MB. A WAL segment rotation creates a checkpoint, but the old WAL segment is not yet GC'd. How many WAL segments can accumulate before recovery time exceeds the 30 s target? What is the exact computation, and what configuration parameter controls the upper bound? — §§13, 8.10, 6.2, 4.3

  14. Fuzzing coverage and WAL parser: The cargo-fuzz target wal_replay generates a malformed WAL entry with a valid CRC32c but a len field that claims 4 GB of payload. What is the expected behavior, what bounds check must exist, and which ADR/principle governs the safe deserialization strategy? — §§13, ADR-17, ADR-04, 6.12

  15. Differential harness false negative: The differential bench (HNSW vs brute-force kNN) reports recall@10 = 1.0 for all fixture queries. An operator incorrectly concludes the HNSW is perfect. What are three plausible ways this metric can be misleading, and what additional tests would expose the issue? — §§25, 6.14, 8.16, 13

  16. Snapshot consistency window: mem-bench conformance runs a snapshot while 50 concurrent remember() calls are in flight. The snapshot export reads records.bin via hard-link. Is the snapshot consistent, and what is the consistency model? What happens if a record is inserted after the hard-link but before the WAL checkpoint is captured? — §§13, 6.2, 7.1, 25

  17. MMR with λ=0 edge case: A Query sets mmr_lambda = 0.0. Describe the mathematical behavior of MMR scoring. What does the final ranked set contain relative to a λ=1.0 result, and what is the practical use case? — §§16.2, 6.4, 8.14, PB5

  18. Provenance chain deletion cascade: A record R is forget()-ed. R appears in Provenance.derived_from of 15 other records (it was a source for 15 reflections). What does the system do with those 15 records? Is there a cascade delete? What invariant governs the answer? — §§21, 7.2, 6.8, 20

  19. Tokio runtime blocking under HNSW insert: A user calls remember() from within an async context. The HNSW insert is CPU-bound and takes 50 ms. Explain why this violates Tokio's cooperative scheduling contract and what the correct implementation pattern is, referencing the specific Tokio primitive. — §§15, ADR-03, 6.9, 8.13

  20. Metrics cardinality explosion: An operator instruments a custom metric mem_retrieve_score{memory_id} with one label per retrieved MemoryId. At 1M records with 10 QPS, how many unique label sets accumulate in the Prometheus scrape target per day, and why does this violate ADR-13? — §§24, ADR-13, 6.6, 12

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Alt-1 — Postgres-Backed Memory Store

Architecture sketch:

Show the original text diagram
Agent
  └─ remember() / recall()
       └─ mem-api
            ├─ Postgres WAL (ACID, MVCC) ← records, provenance, salience
            ├─ pgvector extension          ← HNSW ANN inside Postgres
            └─ pg full-text search        ← tsvector / GIN BM25-like index

Regime where this wins: Small teams (<5 engineers), corpus ≤ 5M records, existing Postgres expertise, SLA on recall latency ≥ 50 ms p99, and no embedded-library constraint. A Postgres-backed design eliminates custom WAL maintenance, provides SQL for flexible migration tooling, and pgvector's HNSW matches Faiss quality at moderate scale.

Real system using this approach: getzep/graphiti (Zep v2) — Postgres + pgvector for fact graph; production deployments with agent memory.

Why rejected here: This design's embedding constraint (co-located library, no separate process) and latency target (p99 recall = 25 ms) are incompatible with Postgres round-trip overhead (typically 1–5 ms per query on local socket, 10–30 ms over network). Additionally, Postgres's MVCC and lock model is over-engineered for the single-writer WAL pattern; the custom WAL's OP_CONSOLIDATED and OP_EVICT operation types have no natural SQL analog without complex triggers.

Alt-2 — Pure In-Process SQLite with WAL Mode

Architecture sketch:

Show the original text diagram
Agent
  └─ remember() / recall()
       └─ mem-api
            └─ SQLite (WAL mode, memory-mapped, single-file .db)
                 ├─ records table (content, salience, provenance)
                 └─ FTS5 virtual table (BM25 natively supported)
            (ANN: external hnswlib FFI linked in-process)

Regime where this wins: Projects that need ACID durability, crash recovery, and full-text BM25 (FTS5 is mature) without maintaining a custom WAL. SQLite WAL mode achieves concurrent readers + single writer with O(1) recovery time. FTS5 provides production BM25+ scoring. Deployment simplicity: single .db file.

Real system using it: The majority of mobile/desktop apps with local memory (iOS Core Data SQLite backend, Android Room). Not used in agent memory specifically, but SQLite's WAL is battle-tested at far larger scale than this system requires.

Why rejected here: SQLite's schema is a fixed-column relational model. MemoryRecord's Vec<f32> embedding cannot be stored natively (must serialize to BLOB and deserialize on every read). FTS5 uses SQLite's own tokenizer pipeline which cannot be swapped for our custom BM25+ implementation without forking SQLite. Most critically, SQLite's WAL provides durability but cannot express the custom operation types (OP_CONSOLIDATED, OP_EVICT) needed for recovery of consolidation and decay state without stored procedures or trigger chains — both of which add invisible complexity.

Alt-3 — RocksDB LSM-Tree as the Storage Backend

Architecture sketch:

Show the original text diagram
Agent
  └─ remember() / recall()
       └─ mem-api
            └─ RocksDB (via rust-rocksdb FFI)
                 ├─ CF: records     ← MemoryRecord (bincode value, MemoryId key)
                 ├─ CF: embeddings  ← EmbeddingId → Vec<f32>
                 ├─ CF: metadata    ← salience, ttl, access_count (separate CF for fast update)
                 └─ CF: wal_ops     ← OP_CONSOLIDATED / OP_EVICT log
            (HNSW: in-process as current design; RocksDB replaces custom WAL+mmap)

Regime where this wins: High sustained write throughput (>10K records/s), large corpus (>50M records), multi-thread write concurrency. RocksDB's LSM-tree with Leveled compaction handles high write amplification gracefully; parallel compaction threads maintain read performance. Column Families allow independent compaction and tiering of hot vs cold data. This is the architecture used by production ML feature stores (Feast, Tecton) at massive scale.

Real system using it: Facebook AI Similarity Search (FAISS) integrations at Meta use RocksDB as the metadata backend for billion-scale vector systems.

Why rejected here: RocksDB is a C++ library accessed via unsafe FFI (rust-rocksdb). This introduces build complexity (CMake, clang, C++ ABI), unsafe code surface, and a dependency that dwarfs the entire rest of the project. The custom WAL gives exact semantics for the 8 operation types this system needs; RocksDB's WAL is implicit and not directly replayable at the application level. At the target scale (≤10M records), RocksDB's throughput advantages are not needed, and its operational complexity (tuning block_cache, write_buffer_size, compaction triggers) would require significant engineering.

Alt-4 — Stateless Memory via External Vector Database

Architecture sketch:

Show the original text diagram
Agent
  └─ remember() / recall()
       └─ mem-api (thin client library)
            ├─ Qdrant / Pinecone / Weaviate (external gRPC service)
            │    └─ Dense vector search + metadata filters + optional BM25
            └─ No local WAL; no local store; provider handles durability

Regime where this wins: Agent frameworks that treat memory as a cloud service, not an embedded library. Horizontal scaling of the vector store is the provider's problem. Teams without Rust experience can use Python clients. Provider SLAs cover durability. Weaviate offers native BM25+vector hybrid search in a single deployment.

Real system using it: LangChain + Pinecone, LlamaIndex + Qdrant — the dominant pattern in the current LLM ecosystem.

Why rejected here: This design's core constraint is an embedded library with co-located storage — no network hop on the hot path. An external vector database adds 5–50 ms per call, making the p99 recall = 25 ms target impossible. Additionally, external services provide no control over WAL semantics, consolidation ordering, or decay/eviction policy — the exact features that differentiate this design from generic RAG pipelines. The custom operation types (OP_CONSOLIDATED, OP_EVICT), the namespace-scoped compaction, and the decay model are all incompatible with a provider-opaque external service.

Deferred Features Register

Features marked PhaseDeferred(...) in §1 are tracked here. Any future work to un-defer must update this table and add a new Phase section to the blueprint.

Feature Deferred Tag First Referenced Building Phase (when un-deferred) Un-defer Trigger
Cross-namespace / cross-agent memory federation PhaseDeferred("15.x") §1.2 System Boundaries, §1.3 Non-Goals Phase 15 (new) — federation protocol + cross-ns query API Two or more agent deployments in the same organization need to share semantic memories without duplicating ingest; operator explicitly requests; namespace isolation guarantees must remain intact for non-federated namespaces
Distributed replication; read-replica snapshot sync PhaseDeferred("16.x") §1.2, §1.4 Intended Deployment Phase 16 (new) — WAL segment streaming, replica promotion, lag monitoring Production deployment requires HA (RTO < 60 s, RPO < 5 s); single-node WAL throughput ceiling reached; operator explicitly provisions replica nodes
Multi-tenant RBAC; namespace-level access control PhaseDeferred("17.x") §1.3, §1.4 Phase 17 (new) — token issuance, permission enforcement at mem-api boundary Platform deploys mem-api to untrusted multi-tenant clients; audit requirement mandates per-namespace access logs beyond the current NsId structural isolation
Push-based streaming ingest; continuous observation stream PhaseDeferred("18.x") §1.3, §1.2 Phase 18 (new) — streaming ingestion interface, backpressure over gRPC stream Agent produces observations at sustained rates >1,000/s and cannot tolerate the observe()-per-record round-trip overhead; requires at-least-once delivery semantics on the ingest stream
Graph-native retrieval (full knowledge-graph traversal) PhaseDeferred("19.x") §1.7, §1.8 Phase 19 (new) — multi-hop personalized-PageRank traversal over the link graph, beyond the depth-limited BFS in §8.15 Retrieval workload requires multi-hop reasoning across MemoryRecord.links (HippoRAG-style, PB7) that the depth=2 BFS in §17.2/§8.15 cannot serve at acceptable recall
Pluggable remote index backend (external ANN service) PhaseDeferred("20.x") §1.7, §1.8 Phase 20 (new) — AnnBackend adapter for an external vector-search service, replacing in-process HNSW on the query hot path Corpus exceeds the single-node HNSW ceiling (~10 M vectors / ~188 M nodes before RAM pressure, per the Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model) and a dedicated managed ANN service is operationally justified

Glossary

Term Definition
access_count u32 field on MemoryRecord; incremented on each retrieval; feeds importance scoring and hot-tier promotion.
as-of query Time-travel retrieval: Query.as_of: Option<Timestamp> restricts result set to records where created_at <= as_of; index must support predicate pushdown.
BM25 Best-Match 25 probabilistic term-weighting scoring function. Inverted index in mem-index evaluates exact/stemmed keyword relevance. Score normalized [0,∞) then mapped to [0,1] via per-namespace max.
ContextBudget { max_tokens: usize, reserved: usize } invariant: assembled prompt context ≤ max_tokens; overflow triggers working→long-term eviction before LLM call.
ConsolidationJob Unit of background work (mem-consolidate): one of Summarize/Reflect/Merge/LinkUpdate. Enqueued by scheduler, executed by worker pool.
consolidation Background pipeline converting episodic memories into higher-fidelity semantic/procedural knowledge via summarization, reflection, and dedup-merge.
decay Continuous salience reduction over time (exponential / power-law parameterizable). mem-forget applies decay on reads and on periodic sweep; salience dropping below eviction threshold triggers tier demotion.
dedup Similarity-based duplicate detection in mem-ingest: embedding cosine + exact content hash; near-duplicates → merge provenance, retain higher salience.
durability invariant A remember() returning Ok is recoverable after crash; WAL fsync must complete before acknowledgement; record store is rebuildable from WAL replay alone.
Embedding Fixed-dimension f32 vector (dim D, provider-determined). Stored in embedding store via EmbeddingId; never inlined in MemoryRecord.
EmbeddingId Content-addressed reference ([u8;32] hash of model-id + float bytes) linking a MemoryRecord to its vector in the embedding store.
embedding-record consistency Invariant: every retrievable record has a valid embedding_ref; index entries and store records never dangle; compaction repairs both atomically in a single WAL batch.
episodic memory MemoryKind::Episodic — time-stamped autobiographical events anchored to a session. High recency weight; ages into semantic over consolidation cycles.
eviction Removal of a record from a tier (working→long-term) or from storage entirely. Every eviction logged with reason: `DecayThreshold
forgetting Controlled removal: TTL expiry, decay-driven eviction, or explicit forget() call. No silent loss invariant: every removal is audit-logged.
fusion (RRF) Reciprocal Rank Fusion — score(d) = Σ_r 1/(k + rank_r(d)) across component result lists; k=60 default; produces single merged ranking without score normalization.
HNSW Hierarchical Navigable Small World graph. ANN index in mem-index; O(log N) insert, approximate k-NN search; configurable M (degree), ef_construction, ef_search.
hybrid retrieval Parallel execution of vector ANN, BM25, recency, and importance sub-queries; results fused via RRF then diversified via MMR before optional rerank.
importance Normalized salience of a record, stored in MemoryRecord.salience: Salience(f32 ∈ [0,1]); fed by LLM-assigned score at ingest and updated by access_count and consolidation.
inverted index BM25 keyword index in mem-index; maps term→posting list (MemoryId, term_freq); used for exact/phrase/stemmed retrieval leg.
LlmIface Abstract trait (defined in mem-core) for LLM backends: summarize, extract_facts, extract_entities, embed, rerank. All calls are async and may fail; no LLM is embedded in the system.
MemError Enum covering all library error variants: `StorageIo
MemoryId struct MemoryId(Ulid) — 128-bit ULID; monotonically increasing, time-sortable; used as primary key in store and stable sort tie-breaker in retrieval.
MemoryKind enum { Working, Episodic, Semantic, Procedural } — controls decay rate, consolidation priority, retrieval boost weights, and tiering policy.
MemoryRecord Core unit of storage; full schema in section 11. Immutable after write except for salience, last_access, access_count, and links (all delta-logged via WAL).
MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance) Diversity filter post-fusion: iteratively selects next result maximizing λ·relevance - (1-λ)·max_similarity_to_selected; λ ∈ [0,1] from Query.
mmap Memory-mapped file I/O for read path in mem-store; pages faulted on access; compaction replaces mmap region atomically (double-buffer rename).
namespace NsId — logical partition; enforces isolation across agents/sessions; all queries, indices, and compaction operations are namespace-scoped.
no-silent-loss invariant Every eviction, decay, or explicit forget is written to audit log before the record is removed from primary storage; crash between log write and store delete → log replay re-issues delete.
observation buffer Short-lived in-memory deque in mem-ingest that batches raw agent observations before extraction and ingestion; bounded capacity; back-pressure when full.
procedural memory MemoryKind::Procedural — how-to knowledge, skills, workflows. Low decay rate; high reuse potential; retrieved on task-matching queries.
Provenance { source: String, session: NsId, derived_from: Vec<MemoryId> } — audit trail for every record; required for reflection lineage and deletion cascades.
RRF See Fusion (RRF).
recency Temporal proximity score: f(t) = exp(-λ·(now - last_access)) or power-law variant; weight blended into RRF via Query.recency_bias.
reflection Higher-order memory synthesis: mem-consolidate batches episodic memories, calls LlmIface::summarize, stores result as new Semantic record with Provenance.derived_from links.
RetrievalResult { record: MemoryRecord, score: f32, components: { vec, bm25, recency, importance } } — score is post-fusion; components retained for observability and re-ranking input.
Salience struct Salience(f32) — clamped [0,1]; primary signal for importance index, tiering, eviction threshold, and context packing priority.
semantic memory MemoryKind::Semantic — general world/domain knowledge, facts, distilled summaries. Lower recency weight; long TTL or none; primary consolidation output.
snapshot Consistent point-in-time copy of store + index: WAL flushed, compaction quiesced, files hard-linked or copied; used for backup and cold-start recovery testing.
time-travel See as-of query.
tiering Three-tier model: hot (working memory, in-process), warm (long-term, fast mmap), cold (archive, compressed). Promotion/demotion driven by mem-forget decay and mem-context eviction.
TTL MemoryRecord.ttl: Option<Duration> — wall-clock expiry after created_at; expired records are candidates for immediate eviction on next sweep or read.
ULID Universally Unique Lexicographically Sortable Identifier; encodes millisecond timestamp + random bits; provides total order without coordination.
WAL Write-Ahead Log in mem-store; append-only; every mutation (insert, salience-update, link-add, delete) serialized as a WAL entry before being applied to the record store; enables crash recovery by replay.
working memory MemoryKind::Working — transient in-session context held in mem-context; highest decay rate; evicted to long-term (or dropped) when context budget exceeded or session ends.

benchmarks/                 — criterion harnesses (integration; cross-crate)
  ingest_throughput.rs      — records/s under varied batch size
  retrieval_latency.rs      — P50/P95/P99 recall latency; k=5/10/20
  consolidation_overhead.rs — background CPU/IO impact during foreground ops
  context_assembly.rs       — token-packing speed; budget overflow handling

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
0 Workspace + crate scaffolding; mem-core types; LlmIface trait; CI pipeline 1 week ULID/serde compatibility; trait object dyn safety All types compile; MemoryId monotonic test passes; CI green
1 mem-store: WAL writer/reader, record store, mmap, crash recovery 2 weeks fsync semantics on macOS/Linux; mmap page fault correctness Recovery round-trip test; WAL replay rebuilds exact record set
2 mem-embed: EmbedProvider trait, chunkers, embedding cache 1 week Dim mismatch between providers; cache invalidation on model change Unit tests per chunker; cache hit-rate test; passthrough provider integration
3 mem-index: HNSW + BM25 + recency + importance + meta indices 2 weeks HNSW recall vs speed tradeoff; BM25 IDF drift on deletes nDCG@10 ≥ 0.82 on synthetic; exact match BM25 test; namespace isolation test
4 mem-ingest: observation buffer, extract, dedup, embed, write pipeline 2 weeks LlmIface latency in pipeline; dedup false-positive rate End-to-end ingest test; dedup collision rate < 1%; back-pressure test
5 mem-retrieve: hybrid fusion (RRF), MMR, rerank hook, as-of 2 weeks Score normalization drift across index types; as-of index cost Retrieval determinism test; MMR diversity test; time-travel query test
6 mem-context: working memory, token-budget assembler, eviction 1 week Token counter accuracy; budget overflow edge case Budget invariant fuzz test; overflow eviction integration test
7 mem-consolidate: summarize/reflect/merge/link workers 2 weeks LlmIface cost at scale; reflection loop / infinite derivation Consolidation audit completeness; reflection cycle-detection test
8 mem-forget: decay sweep, TTL, eviction, tiering, archive 1.5 weeks Decay parameter sensitivity; cold-tier read-back latency No-silent-loss audit test; tier promotion/demotion round-trip; TTL expiry test
9 mem-api + mem-telemetry: public API, session mgmt, metrics, tracing 1.5 weeks API surface stability; telemetry overhead on hot path remember/recall/forget/reflect integration test; p99 telemetry overhead < 2%
10 mem-cli: all commands; TOML config; snapshot create/restore 1 week CLI surface freeze; snapshot integrity on large stores CLI smoke tests; snapshot restore produces identical stats
11 mem-bench: retrieval quality, soak harness, criterion benches 1.5 weeks Benchmark noise on CI; dataset licensing nDCG@10 regression gate; soak 24 h no memory leak; p99 retrieve < 50 ms at 1 M records
12 Hardening: fuzz corpus, property tests, chaos recovery, doc 2 weeks Undiscovered WAL edge cases; HNSW graph corruption on partial write Fuzz coverage on WAL/codec; chaos test: kill-9 mid-ingest → full recovery; docs complete
13 Release: version tags, CHANGELOG, benchmark baseline published 0.5 weeks Semver surface breaks discovered late 1.0.0-rc tag; all CI gates green; benchmark baseline committed to repo
